Yefremov) represented a liberal-reformist direction in Soviet non-realist literature, they could not be classified as dissidents or anti-Soviet; they demonstrated loyalty to the authorities until their final years.<sup about="#mwt155" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-43" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-43&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;refn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Refn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Critic Roman Arbitman wrote in the preface to the 33-volume complete works: \&quot;In reality, the writers were neither ardent dissidents — despite 'The Ugly Swans' published by the émigré 'Posev' — nor, even less, pillars of the ruling regime — despite the consistent presence of the words 'communist' and 'communard' in their novels about a bright future. The writers simply tried to live honestly, without compromising themselves, within the given conditions and extracted the maximum from the few degrees of freedom granted to them at the time by the Homeostatic Universe\&quot;.&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-43" data-mw-group="Notes" id="mwAVs"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAVw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAV0">[</span>Notes 5<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAV4">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt158" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20157_44-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20157&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20157-44&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2015&quot;},&quot;p&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;7&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20157-44" id="mwAV8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAWA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAWE">[</span>39<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAWI">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt161" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKomissarov2017100_45-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEKomissarov2017100&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEKomissarov2017100-45&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Komissarov&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2017&quot;},&quot;p&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;100&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKomissarov2017100-45" id="mwAWM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAWQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAWU">[</span>40<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAWY">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAWc">In the creative tandem, Arkady Natanovich and Boris Natanovich switched roles over time. According to the younger brother, in the decade from 1955 to 1965, the elder — Arkady — "was... assertive, incredibly hardworking, and fearless of any work in the world. Probably, after the army, this civilian world seemed to him a realm of unlimited freedoms and incredible opportunities." It is noted that, twenty years later, leadership (in terms of plot and publishing initiatives) clearly shifted to Boris Strugatsky.<sup about="#mwt164" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20204_46-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20204&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20204-46&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;p&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;4&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2020&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20204-46" id="mwAWg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAWk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAWo">[</span>41<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAWs">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt167" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMezhuev2009_47-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEMezhuev2009&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEMezhuev2009-47&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Mezhuev&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2009&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMezhuev2009-47" id="mwAWw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAW0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAW4">[</span>42<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAW8">]</span></span></a></sup> By early 1991, a breakdown occurred, when, in Ant Skalandis's words, "it became completely impossible to combine what they were now inventing and writing".<sup about="#mwt170" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008614_48-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTESkalandis2008614&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008614-48&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Skalandis&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2008&quot;},&quot;p&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;614&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008614-48" id="mwAXA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAXE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAXI">[</span>43<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAXM">]</span></span></a></sup> The last meeting of Arkady and Boris Strugatsky took place in Moscow from 16 to 19 January 1991, and ended in a serious quarrel, possibly related to the elder brother's illness.<sup about="#mwt173" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-49" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-49&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;refn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Refn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;In A. N. Strugatsky's diary entry dated 20 January 1991, he wrote: \&quot;Tired of Boris. It feels like he comes to gorge on meat and watch videos and TV. Toward the end, we quarreled. &lt;...&gt; I was beside myself with anger. I can't deal with him anymore. He makes me ill. He plans to show up in the second half of February, but I won't let him. &lt;...&gt; B. N., my health is more important than you. After his departure, I even had something like a breakdown...\&quot;&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-49" data-mw-group="Notes" id="mwAXQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAXU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAXY">[</span>Notes 6<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAXc">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt176" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020416_50-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020416&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020416-50&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2020&quot;},&quot;p&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;416&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020416-50" id="mwAXg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAXk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAXo">[</span>44<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAXs">]</span></span></a></sup> However, in the working diary kept by both co-authors, the entry "The writer 'Br<span typeof="mw:Entity" id="mwAXw">&lt;</span>others&gt; Strugatsky' no longer exists" was made by B. N. Strugatsky on 13 October — the day after his brother's death.<sup about="#mwt179" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008635_51-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTESkalandis2008635&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008635-51&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Skalandis&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2008&quot;},&quot;p&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;635&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008635-51" id="mwAX0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAX4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAX8">[</span>45<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAYA">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt182" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020177,_403_52-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020177, 403&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020177,_403-52&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;177, 403&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2020&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020177,_403-52" id="mwAYE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAYI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAYM">[</span>46<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAYQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="7" id="mwAYU" aria-labelledby="Literary_technique:_collaborative_work"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Literary_technique:_collaborative_work">Literary technique: collaborative work</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Literary technique: collaborative work"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwAYY">The collaboration of two brothers living and working in different cities distinguished the Strugatskys in terms of literary technique. In interviews, the writers joked about it, leading to a legend that they met to work at the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bologoye" title="Bologoye" id="mwAYc">Bologoye</a> station, halfway between Leningrad and Moscow. In reality, developing methods for collaborative creativity took over a decade and was finalized by 1960. For a long time, the initiator of the creative tandem's projects was Arkady Natanovich, who, during his military service (in Siberia, Kamchatka, and the Far East), tried his hand at writing stories and novellas, while Boris Natanovich acted as the first reader, reviewer, and consultant on "technical issues." In the second half of the 1950s, when Arkady Strugatsky was demobilized and worked as an editor in various Moscow publishing houses, the brothers did not abandon the idea of collaborative creativity. However, the technique used during the writing of <i id="mwAYg">the Land of Crimson Clouds</i> is described by A. V. Snigirev as "strange": the co-authors wrote independently, sending each other completed chapters, fragments, and parts. This technique was perceived by the Strugatskys themselves as "flawed" as early as their 1956 correspondence. Later, Boris Natanovich often acted as the "idea generator," with Arkady implementing those ideas in the text, though the elder brother insisted that they should write together: "The firm '<abbr title="Acronymic abbreviation: &quot;Arkady and Boris Strugatsky&quot;." about="#mwt185" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:ExpandedAttrs" id="mwAYo" data-mw="{&quot;attribs&quot;:[[{&quot;txt&quot;:&quot;title&quot;},{&quot;html&quot;:&quot;&lt;span typeof=\&quot;mw:Nowiki\&quot; id=\&quot;mwAYk\&quot;&gt;Acronymic abbreviation: \&quot;Arkady and Boris Strugatsky\&quot;.&lt;/span&gt;&quot;}]],&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;abbr&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Abbr&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;ABS&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Acronymic abbreviation: \&quot;Arkady and Boris Strugatsky\&quot;.&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;0&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}">ABS</abbr>' must act as a united front".<sup about="#mwt187" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZerkalov19917_53-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEZerkalov19917&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEZerkalov19917-53&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Zerkalov&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1991&quot;},&quot;p&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;7&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZerkalov19917-53" id="mwAYs"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAYw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAY0">[</span>47<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAY4">]</span></span></a></sup></p><p id="mwAY8">

In 1958, the co-authors tried and rejected the concept of "stepped editing," where Arkady sent Boris chapters as they were completed, receiving them back after editing. At the same time, the brothers experimented with different genres and forms: the elder in novels, the younger in short stories. Sometimes they switched roles. In 1955, they wrote the story <i id="mwAZA">Sand Fever</i>, created spontaneously, like <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bouts-Rimés" title="Bouts-Rimés" id="mwAZE">bouts-rimés</a>, without any preliminary plan, yet resulting in a cohesive work.<sup about="#mwt190" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20205,_119_54-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20205, 119&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20205,_119-54&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;5, 119&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2020&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky20205,_119-54" id="mwAZI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAZM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAZQ">[</span>48<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAZU">]</span></span></a></sup> In addition to working on the text, communicating with journal editors and publishing house management, conducting ongoing correspondence, and attending business meetings caused conflicts over the division of responsibilities as early as the 1960s, when the brothers were actively publishing and became sought-after writers. The collaborative work method was finalized either by the completion of The Land of Crimson Clouds or during the writing of the novella <i id="mwAZY"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Way_to_Amalthea" title="The Way to Amalthea" id="mwAZc">The Way to Amalthea</a></i> in 1959. In the retrospective <i id="mwAZg"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comments_on_the_Past" title="Comments on the Past" id="mwAZk">Comments on the Past</a></i>, Boris Strugatsky stated that, having tried all conceivable schemes and variants of working together, the co-authors, through trial and error, created a final technique that they adhered to until the end of their creative tandem.<sup about="#mwt193" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky202019_55-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky202019&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky202019-55&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;p&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;19&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2020&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky202019-55" id="mwAZo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAZs"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAZw">[</span>49<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAZ0">]</span></span></a></sup></p><blockquote id="mwAZ4"><p id="mwAZ8">The specificity of <abbr title="Acronymic abbreviation: &quot;Arkady and Boris Strugatsky&quot;." about="#mwt196" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:ExpandedAttrs" id="mwAaE" data-mw="{&quot;attribs&quot;:[[{&quot;txt&quot;:&quot;title&quot;},{&quot;html&quot;:&quot;&lt;span typeof=\&quot;mw:Nowiki\&quot; id=\&quot;mwAaA\&quot;&gt;Acronymic abbreviation: \&quot;Arkady and Boris Strugatsky\&quot;.&lt;/span&gt;&quot;}]],&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;abbr&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Abbr&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;ABS&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Acronymic abbreviation: \&quot;Arkady and Boris Strugatsky\&quot;.&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;0&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}">ABS</abbr>'s work, where any minimally serious text is created together, simultaneously, word by word, paragraph by paragraph, page by page; where any phrase in the draft has two, three, or four predecessor phrases proposed as variants, once spoken aloud but never written down; where the final text is a fusion of two — sometimes very different — visions of it, not just a fusion but a kind of chemical compound at the molecular level — this specificity, among other things, produces two purely quantitative consequences. Firstly, the amount of paper in the archives is reduced to a minimum. Each novel exists in the archive as only one, at most two, drafts, each of which is actually a recorded, edited, and compressed text of two, three, or four ORAL drafts, previously articulated by the authors and polished through fierce discussions.<sup about="#mwt198" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020118_56-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020118&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020118-56&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Strugatsky&quot;},&quot;p&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;118&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2020&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrugatskyStrugatsky2020118-56" id="mwAaI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAaM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAaQ">[</span>50<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAaU">]</span></span></a></sup></p></blockquote><p id="mwAaY">Relatively short working meetings (initially at the co-authors' homes, and after joining the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Soviet_Writers" title="Union of Soviet Writers" id="mwAac">Union of Soviet Writers</a>, at Writers' Creative Houses) were preceded by extensive material preparation. Work began only after a preliminary development of the work's plan, sometimes down to minute details (including the arrangement of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mise-en-scène" title="Mise-en-scène" id="mwAag">mise-en-scènes</a> or even key phrases).<sup about="#mwt201" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZerkalov19917–8_57-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEZerkalov19917–8&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEZerkalov19917–8-57&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Zerkalov&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1991&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;7–8&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZerkalov19917–8-57" id="mwAak"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAao"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAas">[</span>51<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAaw">]</span></span></a></sup> The writing process, in B. N. Strugatsky's words, was like "building flesh on the skeleton": the text was the result of a continuous dialogue, discussing literally every word and phrase. Further work with the text, related to passing through editorial and censorship, was also intensive, though B. Strugatsky often evaluated it negatively. However, according to A. V. Snigirev, published draft and preparatory materials suggest that editors' and censors' suggestions significantly improved the texts both artistically and ideologically, contributing to more precise work on each word and the overall content. According to Snigirev, the Strugatskys' "internal" texts or those not intended for publication clearly demonstrate a certain "weakness" compared to published works.</p>

</section></section><section data-mw-section-id="8" id="mwAa0" aria-labelledby="Literary_features"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Literary_features">Literary features</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Literary features"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>

<section data-mw-section-id="9" id="mwAa4" aria-labelledby="Pretexts_and_the_writers'_self-myth"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Pretexts_and_the_writers'_self-myth"><span id="Pretexts_and_the_writers.27_self-myth" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>Pretexts and the writers' self-myth</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Pretexts and the writers' self-myth"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwAa8">At the turn of the 2000s, Boris Strugatsky began extensive work to explain the creative process he shared with his brother, but the <i id="mwAbA">Comments on the Past</i> and responses to readers' questions do not always align with surviving and published documents, sometimes representing, in the terminology of A. V. Snigirev, memory's games. Furthermore, it becomes clear that the history of the creation and publication of many of the Strugatskys' works, particularly their debut novella <i id="mwAbE">The Land of Crimson Clouds</i>, was, to some extent, deliberately obscured by Boris Strugatsky himself. Similarly, the story that the brothers began writing their debut novella on a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gambling" title="Gambling" id="mwAbI">bet</a> with Arkady Strugatsky's wife has become legendary. The co-authors kept work diaries irregularly, and many surviving records are terse and barely lend themselves to interpretation. As noted by D. Volodihin and G. Prashkevich, the "<i id="mwAbM">Comments on the Past</i> represent a narrative... from the lesser part of this creative partnership".<sup about="#mwt204" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201240–42_58-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201240–42&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201240–42-58&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Volodihin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Prashkevich&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;40–42&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2012&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201240–42-58" id="mwAbQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAbU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAbY">[</span>52<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAbc">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt207" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESnigirev2015176_59-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTESnigirev2015176&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESnigirev2015176-59&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Snigirev&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2015&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;176&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESnigirev2015176-59" id="mwAbg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAbk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAbo">[</span>53<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAbs">]</span></span></a></sup> Candidate of Philological Sciences A. V. Snigirev described the self-myth of the writer, which began forming in the early 1990s, closely tied to <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography" id="mwAbw">hagiographical</a> literature and the genre of the "literary monument." The scale of the self-myth created by Boris Strugatsky is likely the largest in contemporary Russian literature and can be compared to the "<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._P._Lovecraft" title="H. P. Lovecraft" id="mwAb0">Lovecraftian</a> myth" in the United States. A key component of this myth is the categorical denial of any <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intertextuality" title="Intertextuality" id="mwAb4">intertextual</a> connections, with the works presented exclusively as original. For example, in their early period, the Strugatskys willingly mentioned Ivan Yefremov's name and even attempted, at the turn of the 1950s–1960s, to construct a unified meta-world of the Bright Future, using the same setting elements and terminology, primarily based on Yefremov's works.<sup about="#mwt215" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-60" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-60&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-60" id="mwAb8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAcA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAcE">[</span>54<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAcI">]</span></span></a></sup> Conversely, when their vision of Soviet science fiction began to diverge, Boris Strugatsky spoke and wrote about Ivan Antonovich in a dismissive tone ("he was not a great writer, nor did he particularly claim that title—he considered himself primarily a philosopher").<sup about="#mwt210" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-61" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-61&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;refn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Refn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Conversely, D. Volodihin and G. Prashkevich, characterizing the stylistics of their work, stated that early Yefremov is as much more dynamic and lively than the early Strugatskys as the mature Strugatskys (from the mid-1960s) are literarily more refined than the mature Yefremov.&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-61" data-mw-group="Notes" id="mwAcM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAcQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAcU">[</span>Notes 7<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAcY">]</span></span></a></sup></p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwAcc"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Stanisław_Lem.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwAcg"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Stanisław_Lem.jpg" src="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Stanis%C5%82aw_Lem.jpg/250px-Stanis%C5%82aw_Lem.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Stanis%C5%82aw_Lem.jpg/500px-Stanis%C5%82aw_Lem.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1364" data-file-type="bitmap" height="341" width="250" class="mw-file-element" id="mwAck"></a><figcaption id="mwAco"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanisław_Lem" title="Stanisław Lem" id="mwAcs">Stanisław Lem</a> in 1966</figcaption></figure>
<p id="mwAcw">The Strugatskys' work is characterized by the constant presence of pretexts that served as models for plot construction, shaping ideological concepts, stylistic guidelines, and a means to overcome creative crises. "The Strugatskys were quite comfortable with using other texts in their works,"<sup about="#mwt216" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESnigirev2021210_62-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTESnigirev2021210&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESnigirev2021210-62&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Snigirev&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2021&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;210&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESnigirev2021210-62" id="mwAc0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAc4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAc8">[</span>55<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAdA">]</span></span></a></sup> extensively employing intertextual references and literary games. Critics of the Strugatskys' early works noted significant influence (and even possible borrowings) from <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanisław_Lem" title="Stanisław Lem" id="mwAdE">Stanisław Lem</a>. In later comments, Boris Strugatsky spoke only of a "great similarity of mentalities," categorically denying any possibility of influence. However, the pretexts for <i id="mwAdI">The Land of Crimson Clouds, Attempt at Escape,</i> and <i id="mwAdM">Snail on the Slope</i> were Lem's <i id="mwAdQ">Astronauts, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eden_(Lem_novel)" title="Eden (Lem novel)" id="mwAdU">Eden</a>, and Manuscript Found in a Bathtub</i>, respectively, with translations (sometimes in manuscript form) known to the Strugatskys before they began work on these texts.<sup about="#mwt219" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESnigirev2021210_62-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTESnigirev2021210&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESnigirev2021210-62&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Snigirev&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2021&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;210&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESnigirev2021210-62" id="mwAdY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAdc"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAdg">[</span>55<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAdk">]</span></span></a></sup> In writing <i id="mwAdo">Snail on the Slope</i>, familiarity with the works of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Kafka" title="Franz Kafka" id="mwAds">Kafka</a> played a significant role, as <i id="mwAdw"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Trial" title="The Trial" id="mwAd0">The Trial</a></i> was read by A. N. Strugatsky in 1963 in English translation; the Russian translation appeared in 1965. On this basis, B. Strugatsky acknowledged the influence of Kafkaesque imagery on the depictions of the Administration, but dated his familiarity with Kafka to after <i id="mwAd4">Snail on the Slope</i> was published. Analysis by A. V. Snigirev demonstrates a much stronger connection between <i id="mwAd8">Snail</i>... and Lem's <i id="mwAeA">Manuscript Found in a Bathtub</i>. M. F. Amusin argued that the similarity between the Strugatskys' early work and Lem up to <i id="mwAeE">Solaris</i> is undeniable but thematic in nature. It primarily concerns a tendency toward global generalizations, thought experiments, and the sociocultural orientation of these experiments. However, Lem's <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemology" title="Epistemology" id="mwAeI">epistemological</a> pursuits after the 1960s, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodernism" title="Postmodernism" id="mwAeM">postmodernist</a> games, construction of informational models of human consciousness, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiotics" title="Semiotics" id="mwAeQ">semiotic</a> explorations significantly diverged the writers from one another. The Strugatskys remained within the realm of social practice and the psychological and behavioral attitudes of their characters.<sup about="#mwt222" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996130_63-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996130&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996130-63&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;130&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996130-63" id="mwAeU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAeY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAec">[</span>56<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAeg">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAek">A significant layer of pretexts for the Strugatskys lies in American science fiction from the 1950s–1970s. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_Bradbury" title="Ray Bradbury" id="mwAeo">Ray Bradbury</a> had a certain influence on the co-authors, noticeable, according to M. Amusin, in <i id="mwAes">Predatory Things of the Century.</i> The writers were united by a love for building fantastic premises on the solid foundation of everyday realities and a reverence for life in all its manifestations. However, Bradbury's mythopoetic <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suggestiveness" title="Suggestiveness" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAew">suggestiveness</a>, his pronounced <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism" title="Romanticism" id="mwAe0">romanticism</a>, and emotionality are far from the Strugatskys' realistic science fiction. The brothers' artistic logic is stricter, more rationalistic, rooted in the sphere of social relations. Experimenting with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegory" title="Allegory" id="mwAe4">allegories</a>, the Strugatskys used them for <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satire" title="Satire" id="mwAe8">satirical</a> purposes without exceeding the boundaries of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grotesque" title="Grotesque" id="mwAfA">grotesque</a>. Their satirical tone aligned them with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Sheckley" title="Robert Sheckley" id="mwAfE">Robert Sheckley</a>. Thematically, his story <i id="mwAfI">The Hangover</i> correlates with <i id="mwAfM">Predatory Things of the Century</i>, though it lacks the Strugatskys' sharpness and radical fervor. Even closer to the co-authors is <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurt_Vonnegut" title="Kurt Vonnegut" id="mwAfQ">Kurt Vonnegut</a>, though he lacks their rationality in posing problems and tendency toward model situations. They are united by the consistent use of fantastic premises to diagnose the ailments of human civilization and a rejection of isolationism that relegates science fiction to a "ghetto," a preserve of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escapism" title="Escapism" id="mwAfU">escapism</a>. The Strugatskys' fantastic epic about the conquest of the universe is comparable in many ways to the worlds of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ursula_K._Le_Guin" title="Ursula K. Le Guin" id="mwAfY">Ursula K. Le Guin</a>, with a fundamental difference: the American writer views progressor activity as a given, requiring neither "feverish justification nor passionate refutation".<sup about="#mwt225" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996131–133_64-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996131–133&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996131–133-64&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;131–133&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996131–133-64" id="mwAfc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAfg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAfk">[</span>57<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAfo">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt228" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-66" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-66&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;refn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Refn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Unlike Lem and Le Guin, the Strugatskys never attempted to model alternative biological forms of intelligent life or a consistent picture of a society built on such a biological basis. With rare exceptions, their aliens are indistinguishable from humans in biosocial terms, differing only in the flow and forms of historical time. The only complete, consistent depiction of an extraterrestrial civilization is presented in the forest chapters of \&quot;Snail on the Slope.\&quot; M. Amusin noted that Le Guin and most of her colleagues focused readers' attention on the \&quot;distant,\&quot; exotic, and incomprehensible. Her Ekumen is simultaneously characterized by centripetal force and atomization; the worldview is conveyed through complex mythopoetic metaphors and intricate symbolism. The Strugatskys' universe, in worldview terms, is Ptolemaic—it gravitates toward and revolves around humanity. Their writerly approach is fundamentally anti-transcendental and anti-mythological, lacking references to higher, universal entities; the most complex problems are conceived as intellectually graspable, tangible, and observable.{{sfn|Amusin|1996|pp=134–135}}&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-66" data-mw-group="Notes" id="mwAfs"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAfw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAf0">[</span>Notes 8<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAf4">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAf8">Yu. Chernyakhovskaya noted that even in their very first novella, <i id="mwAgA">The Land of Crimson Clouds,</i> the influence of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Hemingway" title="Ernest Hemingway" id="mwAgE">Hemingway</a> is evident, which in the first half of the 1960s led to complex combinations in the co-authors' work. The cult of camaraderie and drinking, set by Hemingway's texts, aligned well with the values of the Russian intelligentsia (and Arkady Strugatsky's personal inclinations) and gave rise to a specific hybrid — "romantic ignorance," where outward roughness masked an inner reservoir of knowledge. This did not negate the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pathos" title="Pathos" id="mwAgI">pathos</a> of humanity's struggle with nature and the "calm heroism" of the main characters, as it was through Hemingway's texts that the Strugatskys learned to eschew pathos.<sup about="#mwt234" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016153_67-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016153&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016153-67&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Chernyakhovskaya&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2016&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;153&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016153-67" id="mwAgM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAgQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAgU">[</span>59<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAgY">]</span></span></a></sup> From the 1960s, the elder Strugatsky was strongly influenced by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Bulgakov" title="Mikhail Bulgakov" id="mwAgc">Bulgakov</a>, and thus the later works of the brothers are marked by clear literary rivalry with the author of <i id="mwAgg"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Master_and_Margarita" title="The Master and Margarita" id="mwAgk">The Master and Margarita</a></i> (especially in <i id="mwAgo">Lame Fate</i> and <i id="mwAgs">Burdened by Evil</i>).<sup about="#mwt237" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMezhuev2012_68-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEMezhuev2012&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEMezhuev2012-68&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Mezhuev&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2012&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMezhuev2012-68" id="mwAgw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAg0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAg4">[</span>60<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAg8">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="10" id="mwAhA" aria-labelledby="Authorial_Narrative_Strategy:_Prefiguration"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Authorial_Narrative_Strategy:_Prefiguration">Authorial Narrative Strategy: Prefiguration</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Authorial Narrative Strategy: Prefiguration"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwAhE">One of the distinctive features of the Strugatskys' <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrative" title="Narrative" id="mwAhI">narrative</a> was its encrypted nature, termed by S. Plekhanov as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aesopian_language" title="Aesopian language" id="mwAhM">aesopian language</a>, linked to the general lack of freedom of expression in the Soviet era.<sup about="#mwt248" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-69" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-69&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-69" id="mwAhQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAhU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAhY">[</span>61<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAhc">]</span></span></a></sup> This gave rise to a significant tradition in literary studies and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fandom" title="Fandom" id="mwAhg">fandom</a>, where representatives sought to "decrypt", "decode" the text, determining what certain "strange" or "fantastic" narrative elements "mean" or "symbolize." Most often, the encrypted message is assumed to be an extended ideological statement or even a political manifesto. The consistent application of this methodology from the 1980s to 2010s, while retaining the decryption toolkit, shifted from anti-totalitarian and anti-Soviet subtexts to the search for pro-communist and anti-dissident subtexts. In a politicized environment, attitudes toward the Strugatskys often serve as a form of self-identification, distinguishing "us" from "them," and declaring a wide range of ideological priorities, from left-wing radicalism to <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism" title="Conservatism" id="mwAhk">conservatism</a>. The material for such interpretations typically consists of texts not published in the USSR until the political changes of the 1980s. The interpretation process is perceived and described as "solving a puzzle"—ethical or logical, often in terms of "learning to think"; the Strugatskys are credited with the ability to predict and foresee "our current problems," with contemporaneity itself serving as the key to interpretation.<sup about="#mwt240" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKaspe_70-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEKaspe&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaspe-70&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Kaspe&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaspe-70" id="mwAho"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAhs"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAhw">[</span>62<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAh0">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt243" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMezhuev2012_68-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEMezhuev2012&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEMezhuev2012-68&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Mezhuev&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2012&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMezhuev2012-68" id="mwAh4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAh8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAiA">[</span>60<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAiE">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAiI">According to cultural historian I. Kaspe, attempts at decryption of any kind, regardless of the stated goal, represent the maximum rationalization of the reader's experience: "...the value and (crucially) the possibility of absolutely successful communication between authors and readers is declared. In other words, the reader must ultimately understand what the text means—understanding is inevitable, though delayed for various reasons".<sup about="#mwt249" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-71" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-71&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;refn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Refn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;The question of the Strugatskys' texts' addressivity is considered separately, as the authors themselves rarely indicated it (the only exception being the ironic subtitle to \&quot;Monday Begins on Saturday\&quot;— \&quot;for junior scientific researchers\&quot;). In \&quot;Lame Fate,\&quot; there is a [[deconstruction]] of the reader's image: Felix Sorokin claims he knows nothing about his readers. \&quot;They are just a lot of unfamiliar, completely extraneous people to me.\&quot; Virtually all comprehensive studies of the Strugatskys' work include periodizations based on the observation that works written in different years differ so significantly that they seem addressed to different audiences. Most researchers find the Strugatskys interesting only after they moved beyond \&quot;hard science fiction\&quot; (''hard SF''). [[Lev Losev]] noted (without referencing the Strugatskys' texts) that under the guise of children's or young adult literature, a message for far more sophisticated readers might be concealed.&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-71" data-mw-group="Notes" id="mwAiM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAiQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAiU">[</span>Notes 9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAiY">]</span></span></a></sup> Based on sociological surveys of Strugatsky readers, Irina Kaspe concluded that "the logic of progressive evolution from simple to complex, from unambiguous to polysemous, does not always apply here, and the audience for early and late works may differ far less than the works themselves".<sup about="#mwt252" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKaspe_70-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEKaspe&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaspe-70&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Kaspe&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaspe-70" id="mwAic"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAig"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAik">[</span>62<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAio">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAis">American researcher Yvonne Howell disagreed with the widespread politicized interpretations of the Strugatskys' texts and did not highlight issues of Aesopian language. She considered the strategy of describing the Strugatskys' prose as a set of allegories hidden under the guise of a "popular" (science fiction, partly detective, and adventure) genre to be oversimplified. For the brothers' texts of the 1980s, Howell introduces the concept of the "prefiguration" mechanism: the narrative incorporates images and motifs already established in certain cultural practices, tied to a stable complex of reader expectations and even <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archetype" title="Archetype" id="mwAiw">archetypal</a> motifs (from literary to religious). These motifs can unfold into a specific narrative paradigm, thereby "anticipating" further plot developments to a greater extent than genre formulas. The mechanism operates not so much through <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegory" title="Allegory" id="mwAi0">allegorical</a> means as through intertextuality: according to Howell, the Strugatskys attempt to "renew the interrupted dialogue" with the "philosophical and aesthetic traditions of Russian <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism" title="Modernism" id="mwAi4">modernism</a>," and it is this "apocalyptic culture of the pre- and post-revolutionary era" that is recognized as the primary source of images and motifs, the main channel for prefiguration.<sup about="#mwt255" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowell202130–32_72-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEHowell202130–32&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowell202130–32-72&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Howell&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2021&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;30–32&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowell202130–32-72" id="mwAi8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAjA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjE">[</span>63<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjI">]</span></span></a></sup> Prefiguration implies a highly rationalized authorial strategy. Yvonne Howell emphasizes that the Strugatskys consciously drew on sources "forgotten" by Soviet readers, displaced from "cultural memory." It is assumed that the layering of "half-familiar images" allowed readers to "recall" the forgotten: as the protagonist of the novel "Doomed City" passes by workers digging a pit on the city's outskirts on his way home, the Strugatskys' reader, according to the researcher, should "mentally 'pass by' <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Platonov" title="Andrei Platonov" id="mwAjM">Platonov</a>'s novel <i id="mwAjQ"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Foundation_Pit" title="The Foundation Pit" id="mwAjU">The Foundation Pit</a></i>, recalling both its content and its true 'place' in the disrupted and distorted continuum of Russian intellectual history".<sup about="#mwt258" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowell2021117–119_73-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEHowell2021117–119&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowell2021117–119-73&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Howell&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2021&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;117–119&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowell2021117–119-73" id="mwAjY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAjc"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjg">[</span>64<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjk">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt261" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-74" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-74&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;refn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Refn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Interpretations of allusions and allegories often prove unworkable or misleading. \&quot;Doctor [[Golem]] in ''[[The Ugly Swans]]'' (1967) has absolutely no connection to the [[Kabbalah|Kabbalistic]] legend, and the names of characters in [[Second Invasion from Mars]] (1966) are arbitrarily borrowed from [[Ancient Greek mythology|Greek mythology]]: an aging housewife has nothing in common with the mythological [[Eurydice]], a city intellectual with [[Charon]], a tedious pharmacist with [[Achilles]], and so on. Unlike [[James Joyce|Joyce]]'s [[Ulysses (novel)|''Ulysses'']] or [[John Updike|Updike]]'s ''Centaur'', ''Second Invasion from Mars'' does not imply characterological or plot-related hints. Readers encounter a pseudo-puzzle and simultaneously something recognized as a particular 'atmosphere,' 'aura' of the narrative. The ancient Greek aura may introduce meanings of fate, choice, and tragedy, contrasting with the grotesque story of Martians harvesting human gastric juice on an industrial scale, but it resists further decryption\&quot;.{{sfn|Kaspe}}&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-74" data-mw-group="Notes" id="mwAjo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAjs"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAjw">[</span>Notes 10<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAj0">]</span></span></a></sup> Irina Kaspe highly valued the concept of prefiguration, as "vaguely recognizing but not identifying the prefigured image, the reader attributes to it the characteristics of reality, especially in contrast to 'fantastic' settings and in combination with details 'from everyday life'".<sup about="#mwt267" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKaspe_70-3" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEKaspe&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaspe-70&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Kaspe&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaspe-70" id="mwAj4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAj8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkA">[</span>62<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkE">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAkI">In general, the Strugatskys' creative style, starting with <i id="mwAkM">Attempt at Escape,</i> is characterized by textual multilayeredness and a discrepancy between the initial premise of a work and its final result (as evidenced by published materials and Boris Strugatsky's authorial comments). This allows for interpretations using the formula "this work is about..." while simultaneously avoiding overly definitive judgments. The Strugatskys themselves constantly blended various narrative types: without neglecting entertaining genres, including detective stories, they consistently appealed to serious literature (in Irina Kaspe's terminology, to Western intellectual literature). The authorial strategy manifests at the intersection: while entertaining genres presuppose formulaic reading, motifs and images borrowed from great literature and subjected to prefiguration may be perceived as extraliterary, as "reality itself". M. F. Amusin wrote similarly.<sup about="#mwt270" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKuznetsova2004226_75-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEKuznetsova2004226&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuznetsova2004226-75&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Kuznetsova&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2004&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;226&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuznetsova2004226-75" id="mwAkQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAkU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkY">[</span>65<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkc">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="11" id="mwAkg" aria-labelledby="Themes"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Themes">Themes</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Themes"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwAkk">Mark Amusin identified four genre-thematic groups in the Strugatskys' body of work:<sup about="#mwt273" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996115–116_76-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996115–116&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996115–116-76&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;115–116&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996115–116-76" id="mwAko"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAks"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAkw">[</span>66<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAk0">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<ol id="mwAk4"><li id="mwAk8">Fantastic <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_Movie" title="Epic Movie" id="mwAlA">epic</a> (the space trilogy, <i id="mwAlE">Noon, 22nd Century, Hard to Be a God</i>, and other works about contact).</li>
<li id="mwAlI"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dystopia" title="Dystopia" id="mwAlM">Dystopias</a> and cautionary novels (<i id="mwAlQ">Predatory Things of the Century</i>, <i id="mwAlU">The Ugly Swans</i>, <i id="mwAlY">Roadside Picnic</i>).</li>
<li id="mwAlc">Fantastic <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satire" title="Satire" id="mwAlg">satire</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegory" title="Allegory" id="mwAlk">allegory</a> (<i id="mwAlo">The Doomed City</i>, <i id="mwAls">Second Invasion from Mars</i>, <i id="mwAlw">Snail on the Slope</i>, <i id="mwAl0">Tale of the Troika</i>).</li>
<li id="mwAl4">Fairy-tale fantasy (<i id="mwAl8">Monday Begins on Saturday</i>, <i id="mwAmA">Lame Fate</i>, <i id="mwAmE">Burdened by Evil</i>).</li></ol>

<p id="mwAmI">In terms of events, the works, starting with <i id="mwAmM">The Land of Crimson Clouds</i>, form a unified history of the future, spanning events from the late 20th to the end of the 22nd century. The spatial structure of this world is relatively simple, encompassing realms of worlds already known and yet to be explored by humanity in the conquered cosmos; in early works written before 1962, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansionism" title="Expansionism" id="mwAmQ">expansionist</a> motifs predominate. The main characters here are space explorers: pilots, paratroopers, and later <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressor" title="Progressor" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAmU">progressors</a>. In the works of the 1980s, the motifs shift: humanity's concerns revolve around potential influences on their own planet from inhabitants of other worlds. Even in their earliest epic novellas, the Strugatskys developed several recurring motifs that run through their entire oeuvre. One such motif is the threat posed by non-human intelligence. In the <i id="mwAmY">Noon</i> utopia, the <i id="mwAmc">Massachusetts Nightmare</i> is mentioned: a highly complex cybernetic machine created by humans threatened to create <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence" title="Artificial intelligence" id="mwAmg">artificial intelligence</a>—the foundation of an earthly non-human civilization—in a matter of minutes. Traces of a super-civilization's activities—so-called Wanderers—are found in abundance on the nearest planets of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_System" title="Solar System" id="mwAmk">Solar System</a> and in other worlds.<sup about="#mwt276" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996117–118_77-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996117–118&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996117–118-77&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;117–118&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996117–118-77" id="mwAmo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAms"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAmw">[</span>67<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAm0">]</span></span></a></sup> The Strugatskys were little interested in aliens themselves, and, for example, the biological civilization of the planet Leonida is mentioned in various works in just a couple of lines. In spatiotemporal terms, the Strugatskys' fantastic universe is simple and clearly structured: Earth's affairs are separated from extraterrestrial ones. The theme of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_paradox" title="Time paradox" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAm4">time paradox</a> is present but addressed in a non-cosmic context, primarily in <i id="mwAm8">Attempt at Escape</i> (Saul Repnin, a fugitive from the 20th century) and, in a more veiled form, in <i id="mwAnA">The Ugly Swans</i> (the mokretsy—time travelers from a horrific future who disappear along with it after reality is altered).<sup about="#mwt279" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996120–121_78-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996120–121&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996120–121-78&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;120–121&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996120–121-78" id="mwAnE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAnI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAnM">[</span>68<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAnQ">]</span></span></a></sup> The Strugatskys were minimally interested in the problem of artificial intelligence and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robotics" title="Robotics" id="mwAnU">robotics</a>, which are mentioned only episodically.<sup about="#mwt282" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996122–123_79-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996122–123&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996122–123-79&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;122–123&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996122–123-79" id="mwAnY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAnc"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAng">[</span>69<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAnk">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAno">The archetypal science fiction theme of catastrophe is represented broadly and diversely: In <i id="mwAns">Far Rainbow</i>, the reckless pursuit of physicists for self-sufficient knowledge triggers the Wave, which destroys all life on the planet. The traditional theme of Earth's conquest by aliens is travestied in <i id="mwAnw">Second Invasion from Mars.</i> The catastrophe in <i id="mwAn0">The Ugly Swans i</i>s bloodless and tinged with idealistic tones. <i id="mwAn4">Roadside Picnic</i> presents a fundamental Strugatsky "thought-image": the apocalypse is created by humanity itself, unfolds gradually, and one can learn to live within it. All possibilities of this plot were realized in <i id="mwAn8">Doomed City</i>, whose inhabitants are constantly subjected to both minor and global trials. In "Beetle in the Anthill," a more traditional image of catastrophe is depicted, vividly illustrated by the secondary plot of Lev Abalkin's wanderings with the intelligent cynoid Shchekn on the dead planet Hope.<sup about="#mwt285" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996124–125_80-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996124–125&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996124–125-80&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;124–125&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996124–125-80" id="mwAoA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAoE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAoI">[</span>70<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAoM">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAoQ">Mark Amusin classified a range of dissimilar Strugatsky works as belonging to the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fantasy" title="Fantasy" id="mwAoU">fantasy</a> layer. The novella <i id="mwAoY">Monday Begins on Saturday</i> is a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collage" title="Collage" id="mwAoc">collage</a> of fairy tales and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoscience" title="Pseudoscience" id="mwAog">pseudo-scientific</a> terminology, based on a "handbook of world <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folklore_studies" title="Folklore studies" id="mwAok">folkloristics</a>." One of the key motifs of the novella is omnipotence, which, as it turns out, is severely limited even when resorting to magic. The magician's power is framed by an imperative of continuous self-development, discipline, training, and even the eradication of "Paleolithic remnants in consciousness." Magic punishes the complacent: fur grows on their ears. In the final part of the novella, the Strugatskys present a heuristically vivid yet puzzling model of a scientific problem and its solutions on a deliberately unserious basis: unraveling the mystery of the director of the Institute of Wizardry and Sorcery, Janus Poluektovich (he is "one in two persons"), and his resurrecting and dying parrot named Photon. In the sequel, <i id="mwAoo">Tale of the Troika</i>, the satirical-polemical element is much stronger, with magic serving as a means of illusorily-virtual overcoming of bureaucratic forces.<sup about="#mwt288" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996126–127_81-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996126–127&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996126–127-81&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;126–127&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996126–127-81" id="mwAos"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAow"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAo0">[</span>71<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAo4">]</span></span></a></sup> The fantastical nature of <i id="mwAo8">Lame Fate</i> is rooted in everyday reality, depicted with deliberate realism. However, it fully aligns with the definition given by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzvetan_Todorov" title="Tzvetan Todorov" id="mwApA">Tzvetan Todorov</a>: in 1982 Moscow, utterly improbable events occur, and both the protagonist and readers must constantly waver between explaining them through natural causes or invoking metaphysical entities. The authors left all ambiguities unresolved, transferring them to the level of a fantastic-literary game with the resurrected <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Bulgakov" title="Mikhail Bulgakov" id="mwApE">Mikhail Afanasyevich Bulgakov</a>, who embodies conscience and the true value of literary creation. Fantasy becomes both the goal and the means of the authors' reflection on the essence of literature itself. M. Amusin identified similar motifs in <i id="mwApI">Burdened by Evil</i>, where the narrative unfolds through a matrix of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel" id="mwApM">evangelical</a> events presented as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apocrypha" title="Apocrypha" id="mwApQ">apocrypha</a>; history ceases to be linear.<sup about="#mwt291" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996128_82-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996128&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996128-82&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;128&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996128-82" id="mwApU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwApY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwApc">[</span>72<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwApg">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwApk">The Strugatskys were not interested in science fiction for its own sake, yet the fantastic premise in their works was not utilitarian, used merely for external purposes. All their novellas and stories are linked to the real world, satisfying the same criteria of accuracy and consistency as mainstream prose; moreover, the fantastic premises (in M.
