Amusin's terminology) are "economical" and never serve as mere props. The only exception is <i id="mwApo">Monday Begins on Saturday,</i> where "garish excess" is an element of aesthetic play.<sup about="#mwt294" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996136_83-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996136&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996136-83&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;136&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996136-83" id="mwAps"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwApw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAp0">[</span>73<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAp4">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="12" id="mwAp8" aria-labelledby="Poetics"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Poetics">Poetics</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Poetics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>

<section data-mw-section-id="13" id="mwAqA" aria-labelledby="Plot"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Plot">Plot</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Plot"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwAqE">Mark Amusin noted that the Strugatskys' immense reader popularity is not necessarily an indicator of artistic merit, but, speaking of their works after the 1960s, he describes the co-authors' prose as "at the very least skillful and aesthetically substantial." However, studies of the Strugatskys' poetics by the mid-1990s were complimentary, as science fiction analysis focused solely on "content" and ideological orientation.<sup about="#mwt297" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996139_84-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996139&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996139-84&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;139&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996139-84" id="mwAqI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAqM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAqQ">[</span>74<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAqU">]</span></span></a></sup> The situation has not changed significantly in the 21st century. According to M. Amusin, the Strugatskys were primarily masters of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plot_(narrative)" title="Plot (narrative)" id="mwAqY">plot</a>, capable of constructing event sequences engagingly and elegantly, "maintaining and heightening the degree of reader interest." This skill was honed by the co-authors at the turn of the 1950s–1960s: in <i id="mwAqc">The Land of Crimson Clouds</i>, the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabula" title="Fabula" class="mw-disambig" id="mwAqg">fabula</a> was built linearly by accumulating ever-new difficulties for the heroes to overcome. The fabular line is even weaker in <i id="mwAqk">Return</i> (later reworked as <i id="mwAqo">Noon, 22nd Century</i>) and <i id="mwAqs">Interns</i>, which consist of a mosaic linkage of episodes. In "Interns," a parallel plot follows the maturation of Yura Borodin's personality under the influence of surrounding space explorers, but this line leads to neither <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climax_(literature)" title="Climax (literature)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAqw">climax</a> nor <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_story_structures" title="List of story structures" id="mwAq0">denouement</a>. Subsequently, the Strugatskys learned to subtly accelerate and decelerate the plot, with applied techniques becoming more complex; a "revolution" in plot construction came with the novella <i id="mwAq4">Predatory Things of the Century".</i> Here, they discovered the possibilities of embedding a detective plot within a fantastic narrative, incorporating Hemingwayesque <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reminiscence" title="Reminiscence" id="mwAq8">reminiscences</a>. In this technique, the plot is built on double omission, precise dosing of information provided to the reader, with the reader understanding the depicted reality even less than the protagonist, who must unravel a series of mysteries. This technique was successfully applied in <i id="mwArA">The Ugly Swans, Inhabited Island, Hotel..., and Beetle in the Anthill.</i> The detective line involves not only the use of classic police investigations but, more broadly, the exploration of some hidden essence revealed to the hero through fragmented and random manifestations, only deepening the mystery.<sup about="#mwt300" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996140–141_85-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996140–141&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996140–141-85&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;140–141&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996140–141-85" id="mwArE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwArI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwArM">[</span>75<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwArQ">]</span></span></a></sup> From the time of writing <i id="mwArU">Doomed City,</i> fragmentation became a key compositional principle: this novel consists of six parts, between which the time of action, the protagonist's social status, and the dominant aspects of his worldview change abruptly. Fragmentation worked particularly effectively, according to M. Amusin, in <i id="mwArY">Beetle in the Anthill,</i> where the core investigation plot conducted by Maxim Kammerer is supplemented by Abalkin's reports on wanderings across a dead planet, forming a "novel within a novel". The mysteriousness becomes multi-layered and "multi-storied" as the narrative progresses.<sup about="#mwt303" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996142_86-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996142&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996142-86&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;142&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996142-86" id="mwArc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwArg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwArk">[</span>76<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAro">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwArs">M. Amusin called the Strugatskys' "golden vein" as plot craftsmen their method of complementing the traditional plot goal (reaching a certain point in space or obtaining an object) with an ethical or intellectual conflict crucial to the heroes, requiring resolution during the action. The fabular dynamic, combined with the hero's obligation to make choices, feeds into each other, placing the reader in a "powerful field of anticipation and empathy." The first attempts at such integration were made in <i id="mwArw">Attempt at Escape and Hard to Be a God,</i> and were later used extensively, particularly effectively in <i id="mwAr0">Roadside Picnic" and "Beetle...</i><sup about="#mwt306" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996144_87-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996144&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996144-87&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;144&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996144-87" id="mwAr4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAr8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAsA">[</span>77<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAsE">]</span></span></a></sup> For the mature Strugatskys, open endings are also highly characteristic, of two types. In "Predatory Things of the Century," the plot is cyclical, and the protagonist achieves his goal: he discovers the source of the electronic drug and understands its essence. However, this is only a "minor" ending, as even Ivan Zhilin's immediate superiors are not convinced of his conclusions, and all the work lies ahead. The other type of ending is open to both the reader and the protagonist: typical examples include <i id="mwAsI">Roadside Picnic, A Billion Years Before the End of the World, and The Ugly Swans</i>.<sup about="#mwt309" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996145–146_88-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996145–146&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996145–146-88&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;145–146&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996145–146-88" id="mwAsM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAsQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAsU">[</span>78<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAsY">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="14" id="mwAsc" aria-labelledby="Characters_and_psychology"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Characters_and_psychology">Characters and psychology</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Characters and psychology"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwAsg">The literary heroes of the early Strugatskys were constructed stereotypically, lacking psychological depth,<sup about="#mwt312" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201250–51_89-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201250–51&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201250–51-89&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Volodihin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Prashkevich&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2012&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;50–51&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201250–51-89" id="mwAsk"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAso"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAss">[</span>79<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAsw">]</span></span></a></sup> with individuality often represented by a single trait (even the legendary cosmonaut <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Gorbovsky" title="Leonid Gorbovsky" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAs0">Leonid Gorbovsky</a> was distinguished only by his tendency to rest horizontally), and overcoming functional character construction was achieved only with the figures of Anton-Rumata and Ivan Zhilin, with their inner dialogues and vivid intonation.<sup about="#mwt315" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996146–148_90-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996146–148&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996146–148-90&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;146–148&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996146–148-90" id="mwAs4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAs8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtA">[</span>80<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtE">]</span></span></a></sup> In <i id="mwAtI">The Ugly Swans</i>, the character of writer Viktor Banev becomes, for the first time, a factor in organizing the plot and the narrative itself. This is achieved through the multifaceted manifestation of the hero's personality, as Banev participates in all events and plot twists, which are presented to the reader entirely through his personal perception.<sup about="#mwt318" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996149_91-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996149&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996149-91&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;149&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996149-91" id="mwAtM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAtQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtU">[</span>81<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAtY">]</span></span></a></sup> In the 1970s, the Strugatskys consistently psychologized their characters. M. Amusin considered the stalker Red Schuhart (Roadside Picnic) the most vivid of their heroes, presented from various perspectives: as a narrator, from the viewpoint of his friend Richard Noonan, in the first person, and in the final internal monologue. The novella vividly depicts the hero's aging and the evolution of his personality. Andrei Voronin in <i id="mwAtc">Doomed City</i> is created on the same principle; this novel is notable for the Strugatskys' departure from a single-figure psychological system, supplemented by the character of Izy Katsman, who accompanies Andrei throughout the narrative.<sup about="#mwt321" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996150–151_92-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996150–151&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996150–151-92&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;150–151&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996150–151-92" id="mwAtg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAtk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAto">[</span>82<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAts">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAtw">Initially, the Strugatskys' novellas lacked female characters entirely, but gradually they began to introduce traditionally feminine traits into the Noon Universe (humanity, patience, support, the ability to live for love). The relationship between "female" and "male" is treated uniquely in <i id="mwAt0">Snail on the Slope</i>, where all interactions in the bureaucratic Administration are imbued with sexual motifs, and even the position of Director depends on the favor of the librarian Alevtina. In the Forest, amazons who reproduce via <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthenogenesis" title="Parthenogenesis" id="mwAt4">parthenogenesis</a> dominate, commanding biorobots and destroying technological civilization through a form of <i id="mwAt8">Possession</i>. The authors never depicted the motivations of female characters' actions. Maya Glumova in <i id="mwAuA">The Kid</i> plays an almost equal role to men—Stas or Komov—disrupting a crucial experiment by her colleagues, guided by both emotions and an ethical stance. According to A. V. Kozlov (<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Federal_University" title="Ural Federal University" id="mwAuE">Ural Federal University</a>) and B. Mezhuev, in the Strugatskys' artistic world, the male world is associated with technology and the pathos of universal expansion, opposed by the female world: complete and self-sufficient, not implying development or evolution. Its highest embodiment is the parthenogenetic biological civilization of amazons in <i id="mwAuI">Snail</i>. In <i id="mwAuM">The Kid</i>, the role of a mother saving a child is played by an entire planet, whose secrets Maya Glumova prevents from being penetrated. It is women who reveal to the hero the truth about the impossibility of knowing everything about the world or stepping outside it (to view it from the outside and thus "complete" one's knowledge of it), offering instead the understanding that, while being part of the world, they can vanish from it without a trace.<sup about="#mwt329" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-93" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-93&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-93" id="mwAuQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAuU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAuY">[</span>83<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAuc">]</span></span></a></sup> The only work featuring a female protagonist is <i id="mwAug">The Ugly Swans</i>. Banev's companion, Diana, bears the name of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_(goddess)" title="Diana (goddess)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAuk">Roman goddess of the moon and hunting</a>, reflected in her descriptions: she is full of zeal and not without belligerence. Yvonne Howell, in her dissertation, generalized that female characters in the Strugatskys' works occupy a secondary position—Diana being a rare exception—assigned the role of victim or culprit in bestial, drunken liaisons without love. Only Diana, in the novella's finale, exhibits a divine-mythological essence encoded in her name, making her the only wholly positive female character in the Strugatskys' oeuvre.<sup about="#mwt324" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowell2021147–148_94-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEHowell2021147–148&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowell2021147–148-94&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Howell&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2021&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;147–148&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowell2021147–148-94" id="mwAuo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAus"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAuw">[</span>84<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAu0">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="15" id="mwAu4" aria-labelledby="Style_and_quotation"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Style_and_quotation">Style and quotation</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Style and quotation"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwAu8">From the perspective of the structure of the textual fabric and narrative tone (<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stylistics" title="Stylistics" id="mwAvA">stylistics</a> in the narrow sense), the early Strugatskys' texts up to Hard to Be a God and Monday Begins on Saturday were not distinguished by artistic expressiveness.<sup about="#mwt330" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996153_95-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996153&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996153-95&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;153&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996153-95" id="mwAvE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAvI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAvM">[</span>85<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAvQ">]</span></span></a></sup> The authorial style in The Land of Crimson Clouds lacked brilliance—"calm, smooth, transparent," sometimes bearing the imprint of scientific-technical <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureaucratese" title="Bureaucratese" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAvU">bureaucratese</a>. However, for adventure prose, the text was unhurried, with "an abundance of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Participial_phrases" title="Participial phrases" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAvY">participial phrases</a>, adverbs, adjectives, and a considerable number of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compound_sentences" title="Compound sentences" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAvc">compound sentences</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complex_sentences?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="Complex sentences (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" id="mwAvg" data-mw-i18n="{&quot;title&quot;:{&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;x-page&quot;,&quot;key&quot;:&quot;red-link-title&quot;,&quot;params&quot;:[&quot;Complex sentences&quot;]}}">complex sentences</a> lending the text a solid leisurely pace".<sup about="#mwt333" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201250–51_89-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201250–51&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201250–51-89&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Volodihin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Prashkevich&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;50–51&quot;},&quot;3&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;2012&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVolodihinPrashkevich201250–51-89" id="mwAvk"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAvo"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAvs">[</span>79<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAvw">]</span></span></a></sup> M. Amusin believed that at this stage, the Strugatskys strove not to harm the fantastic plot by spoiling it with "poor, tedious writing," of which there were many examples in Soviet science fiction of the 1950s. Emphasis was placed on restraint, economy, and self-restraint ("Hemingwayesque <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laconicism" title="Laconicism" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAv0">laconicism</a>"), complemented by humor and self-irony. The Strugatskys evolved quickly and, in <i id="mwAv4">Monday...,</i> demonstrated artistic ambitions realized through collage. The collage effect was also used in <i id="mwAv8">Hard to Be a God</i>, where a pastiche of <i id="mwAwA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Three_Musketeers" title="The Three Musketeers" id="mwAwE">The Three Musketeers</a></i> is blended with features of a generalized Middle Ages.<sup about="#mwt336" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996153–155_96-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996153–155&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996153–155-96&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;153–155&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996153–155-96" id="mwAwI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAwM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwQ">[</span>86<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwU">]</span></span></a></sup> Subsequently, the Strugatskys displayed exceptional stylistic and stylistic diversity, sometimes leading to eclecticism as a readiness to "use any available stylistic toolkit." Their most striking creative success is considered <i id="mwAwY">Snail on the Slope</i>.<sup about="#mwt339" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996156_97-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996156&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996156-97&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;156&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996156-97" id="mwAwc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAwg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwk">[</span>87<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAwo">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAws">A fundamental feature of the Strugatskys' prose is its deep literariness. From their earliest works, the authors actively quoted a wide variety of texts, both for aesthetic reasons ("simply beautiful") and to play with erudite readers (who recognize unacknowledged sources), creating an additional effect of perception. Later, the Strugatskys extended the "melting pot effect" to all cultural phenomena. M. Amusin considered <i id="mwAww">Hard to Be a God</i> exemplary in this regard. Anton-Rumata evoked associations with the hero played by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavel_Kadochnikov" title="Pavel Kadochnikov" id="mwAw0">Pavel Kadochnikov</a> in the film <i id="mwAw4">The Scout's Exploit:</i> for 1960s readers, the familiar image facilitated entry into the fantastic world. The range of quotation in <i id="mwAw8">Monday Begins on Saturday</i> is particularly broad, featuring a shape-shifting book that displays different texts each time, from A. N. Tolstoy's <i id="mwAxA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloomy_Morning" title="Gloomy Morning" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAxE">Gloomy Morning</a></i> to Professor Karpov's monograph on the work of the mentally ill. On another level, quotation is conducted through epigraphs, each serving as a commentary on the events of each chapter. The epigraph technique was also used in other works.<sup about="#mwt342" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996165–166_98-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996165–166&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996165–166-98&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;165–166&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996165–166-98" id="mwAxI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAxM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxQ">[</span>88<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAxU">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section></section><section data-mw-section-id="16" id="mwAxY" aria-labelledby="The_Strugatskys_and_Jewishness"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="The_Strugatskys_and_Jewishness">The Strugatskys and Jewishness</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: The Strugatskys and Jewishness"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwAxc">The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shorter_Jewish_Encyclopedia" title="Shorter Jewish Encyclopedia" id="mwAxg">Shorter Jewish Encyclopedia</a> notes that the Strugatskys considered themselves Russian writers, but due to the Jewish origin of their father, many of their works bear traces of national reflection, as well as contemplations on the essence of Jewishness and its role in world history. The character of Izy Katsman (<i id="mwAxk">Doomed City)</i> is described as a "concentration of many characteristic traits of the fate of a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora" title="Jewish diaspora" id="mwAxo">galut</a> Jew"; Israeli critic Maya Kaganskaya found allegories of the position of Jews in the USSR in the novellas <i id="mwAxs">Inhabited Island</i> and <i id="mwAxw"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beetle_in_the_Anthill" title="Beetle in the Anthill" id="mwAx0">Beetle in the Anthill</a>.</i><sup about="#mwt345" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996183_99-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996183&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996183-99&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;183&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996183-99" id="mwAx4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAx8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAyA">[</span>89<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAyE">]</span></span></a></sup> In the novel <i id="mwAyI">Burdened by Evil</i> and the play <i id="mwAyM">Jews of the City of Peter</i> there is a critique of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism" title="Antisemitism" id="mwAyQ">antisemitism</a>, frank to the point of being journalistic.<sup about="#mwt356" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-100" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-100&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-100" id="mwAyU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAyY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAyc">[</span>90<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAyg">]</span></span></a></sup> The most thorough exploration of the theme of Jewishness in the Strugatskys' work was undertaken by Mark Amusin in his 1996 monograph. From the co-authors' earliest works, Strugatsky texts feature numerous characters with Jewish surnames (in <i id="mwAyk"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/From_Beyond_(Strugatsky_novella)" title="From Beyond (Strugatsky novella)" id="mwAyo">From Beyond</a></i>, Captain Kolya Ginzburg and the protagonist, archaeologist Lozovsky; in "Interns," the head of the death-planet laboratory Kolya, speaking in the language of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Babel" title="Isaac Babel" id="mwAys">Babel</a>, and others). This list could be expanded; according to M. Amusin, this was both a tribute to their father and a subtle defiance of the official Soviet thesis of the dissolution of nations under communism.<sup about="#mwt348" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996173–174_101-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996173–174&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996173–174-101&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;173–174&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996173–174-101" id="mwAyw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAy0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAy4">[</span>91<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAy8">]</span></span></a></sup> Many traits of Jewish <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mentality" title="Mentality" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAzA">mentality</a> and spirituality can be found in the character of Peretz in Snail on the Slope: the hero seeks justice, truth, and meaning, while being a social outsider forced to live by the rules of a delirious-grotesque reality. Like many emancipated Jews, Peretz sometimes feels burdened by his freedom-rootlessness, yearning to join a collective or unifying ideology.<sup about="#mwt351" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996176_102-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996176&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996176-102&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;176&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996176-102" id="mwAzE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAzI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAzM">[</span>92<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAzQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwAzU">Examining the concept of progressorism, M. Amusin saw in it the realization of the role of Jews in the global historical process. Progressors are the spiritually advanced vanguard of humanity, transforming inert reality. The image of the progressor combined traits of the "commissar in a dusty helmet" and the scientist in a laboratory catching signals of the Future. In a sense, this resonated with the concept of the "small nation" proposed by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Shafarevich" title="Igor Shafarevich" id="mwAzY">Igor Shafarevich</a>, noted by critics of the Strugatskys from the nationalist camp. However, it is equally clear that the Strugatskys always approached the national question from universal humanistic positions, feeling no need to "defend the honor of Jewishness".<sup about="#mwt357" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996176–177_103-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996176–177&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996176–177-103&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;176–177&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996176–177-103" id="mwAzc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAzg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAzk">[</span>93<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAzo">]</span></span></a></sup> Moreover, changes in their attitude toward the Jewish question directly correlated with shifts in their worldview, and the specifically Jewish theme in their work was never self-sufficient. This is explained by both their <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization" title="Globalization" id="mwAzs">globalist</a> artistic thinking and the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shestidesyatniki" title="Shestidesyatniki" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAzw">shestidesyatniki</a> ideological stance. Jewishness in the Strugatskys' works was a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphor" title="Metaphor" id="mwAz0">metaphor</a> and an archetypal model for representing universal problems.<sup about="#mwt360" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996184–185_104-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996184–185&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996184–185-104&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;184–185&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996184–185-104" id="mwAz4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAz8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA0A">[</span>94<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA0E">]</span></span></a></sup> L. Ashkinazi and A. Kuznetsova clarified that the Jewish theme was not in demand by the Strugatskys' contemporaries, and the authors had no works specifically dedicated to it. This is because the generation of Strugatsky fans was less sensitive to Jewish themes than the previous one, and the co-authors themselves did not consider the topic particularly significant. Jewish motifs and archetypes were present in the authors' active consciousness, but to the same extent, they existed in the consciousness of their readers, meaning the Strugatskys did not convey any guiding idea regarding the Jewish question.<sup about="#mwt365" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-105" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-105&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-105" id="mwA0I"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwA0M"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA0Q">[</span>95<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwA0U">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section></section><section data-mw-section-id="17" id="mwA0Y" aria-labelledby="Works"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Works">Works</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Works"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwA0c"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Andreev_Cover.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwA0g"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Andreev_Cover.jpg" src="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Andreev_Cover.jpg/250px-Andreev_Cover.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Andreev_Cover.jpg/500px-Andreev_Cover.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="722" data-file-type="bitmap" height="181" width="250" class="mw-file-element" id="mwA0k"></a><figcaption id="mwA0o">The first volume of the Strugatskys' 33-volume complete works, published by A. V. Sidorovich Publishing house (2017).</figcaption></figure>
