</section></section><section data-mw-section-id="23" id="mwBcU" aria-labelledby="Adaptations"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Adaptations">Adaptations</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Adaptations"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwBcY">The Strugatsky's books were often adapted for screen, stage, comics, and video games. Some of the adaptations are very loose, like Tarkovsky's <i id="mwBcc">Stalker</i>, some are not adaptations but rather new scripts written by the Brothers themselves, like <i id="mwBcg">The Sorcerers</i>.</p>

<ul id="mwBck"><li id="mwBco"><i id="mwBcs"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalker_(1979_film)" title="Stalker (1979 film)" id="mwBcw">Stalker</a></i> (1979) by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrey_Tarkovsky" title="Andrey Tarkovsky" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBc0">Andrey Tarkovsky</a>, based on the Strugatsky's script, inspired by <i id="mwBc4"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Roadside_Picnic" title="The Roadside Picnic" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBc8">The Roadside Picnic</a></i></li>
<li id="mwBdA"><i id="mwBdE"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_Mountaineer's_Hotel_(film)" title="Dead Mountaineer's Hotel (film)" id="mwBdI">Dead Mountaineer's Hotel</a></i> (1979) by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grigori_Kromanov" title="Grigori Kromanov" id="mwBdM">Grigori Kromanov</a>, based on the novel of the same name</li>
<li id="mwBdQ"><i id="mwBdU"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charodei" title="Charodei" id="mwBdY">The Sorcerers</a></i> (1982), by Konstantin Bromberg, based on the Strugatsky's script inspired by <i id="mwBdc"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monday_Begins_on_Saturday" title="Monday Begins on Saturday" id="mwBdg">Monday Begins on Saturday</a></i></li>
<li id="mwBdk"><i id="mwBdo"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Days_of_Eclipse" title="Days of Eclipse" id="mwBds">Days of Eclipse</a></i> (1988) by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Sokurov" title="Alexander Sokurov" id="mwBdw">Alexander Sokurov</a>, inspired by <i id="mwBd0"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Definitely_Maybe_(novel)" title="Definitely Maybe (novel)" id="mwBd4">One billion years before the end of the world</a></i></li>
<li id="mwBd8"><i id="mwBeA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_to_Be_a_God_(1989_film)" title="Hard to Be a God (1989 film)" id="mwBeE">Hard to be a God</a></i> (1989) by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Fleischmann" title="Peter Fleischmann" id="mwBeI">Peter Fleischmann</a>, based on the novel of the same name</li>
<li id="mwBeM"><i id="mwBeQ">Искушение Б.</i> (<i id="mwBeU">Iskushenie B.</i>) (1991) by Arkadi Sirenko, based on the play <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Spoons_of_Elixir?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="Five Spoons of Elixir (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" id="mwBeY" data-mw-i18n="{&quot;title&quot;:{&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;x-page&quot;,&quot;key&quot;:&quot;red-link-title&quot;,&quot;params&quot;:[&quot;Five Spoons of Elixir&quot;]}}">Five Spoons of Elixir</a> <small id="mwBec">(<a rel="mw:WikiLink/Interwiki" href="https://ru.wikipedia.org/wiki/Искушение%20Б." title="ru:Искушение Б." class="extiw" id="mwBeg">ru</a>)</small></li>
<li id="mwBek"><i id="mwBeo">Nesmluvená setkání</i> (1995 Czech TV movie, English: <i id="mwBes"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unexpected_Encounters?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="Unexpected Encounters (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" id="mwBew" data-mw-i18n="{&quot;title&quot;:{&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;x-page&quot;,&quot;key&quot;:&quot;red-link-title&quot;,&quot;params&quot;:[&quot;Unexpected Encounters&quot;]}}">Unexpected Encounters</a></i>) by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irena_Pavlásková?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="Irena Pavlásková (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" id="mwBe0" data-mw-i18n="{&quot;title&quot;:{&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;x-page&quot;,&quot;key&quot;:&quot;red-link-title&quot;,&quot;params&quot;:[&quot;Irena Pavlásková&quot;]}}">Irena Pavlásková</a>, based on the novel <i id="mwBe4"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Mowgli" title="Space Mowgli" id="mwBe8">Space Mowgli</a></i><sup about="#mwt372" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-108" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-108&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-108" id="mwBfA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBfE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBfI">[</span>98<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBfM">]</span></span></a></sup></li>
<li id="mwBfQ"><i id="mwBfU"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ugly_Swans_(film)" title="The Ugly Swans (film)" id="mwBfY">The Ugly Swans</a></i> (2006) by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_Lopushansky" title="Konstantin Lopushansky" id="mwBfc">Konstantin Lopushansky</a>, based on the novel of the same name</li>
<li id="mwBfg"><i id="mwBfk"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_Planet_(Russian_film)" title="Dark Planet (Russian film)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBfo">Обитаемый остров</a></i> (2008) is a two-part Russian science fiction film directed by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fyodor_Bondarchuk" title="Fyodor Bondarchuk" id="mwBfs">Fyodor Bondarchuk</a>, based on the 1969 novel published in English as <i id="mwBfw"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoners_of_Power" title="Prisoners of Power" id="mwBf0">Prisoners of Power</a></i></li>
<li id="mwBf4"><i id="mwBf8"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zone_(2012_film)" title="Zone (2012 film)" id="mwBgA">Zone</a></i> (2012) by Esa Luttinen, based on the novel <i id="mwBgE">The Roadside Picnic</i></li>
<li id="mwBgI"><i id="mwBgM"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_to_Be_a_God_(2013_film)" title="Hard to Be a God (2013 film)" id="mwBgQ">Hard to be a God</a></i> (2013) by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksei_Yuryevich_German" title="Aleksei Yuryevich German" id="mwBgU">Alexei German</a>, based on the novel of the same name</li></ul>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="24" id="mwBgY" aria-labelledby="Legacy"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Legacy">Legacy</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Legacy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwBgc">Several writers have to a varying degree paid their tribute to the works of Strugatsky brothers:</p>

<ul id="mwBgg"><li id="mwBgk"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Lukyanenko" title="Sergey Lukyanenko" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBgo">Sergey Lukyanenko</a> in his duology <i id="mwBgs"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Stars_Are_Cold_Toys" title="The Stars Are Cold Toys" id="mwBgw">The Stars Are Cold Toys</a></i> has the main character visit a world that is in many aspects strikingly similar to Earth from the Noon Universe but in truth is revealed to be fundamentally different and oppressive. On his website, Lukyanenko commented that he disagreed with the Strugatskys' views on education and upbringing and conceived his duology partly as a polemic criticism of it.<sup about="#mwt375" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Lukyanenko_TSACT_109-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;Lukyanenko_TSACT&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-Lukyanenko_TSACT-109&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-Lukyanenko_TSACT-109" id="mwBg0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBg4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBg8">[</span>99<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBhA">]</span></span></a></sup></li>
<li id="mwBhE">The plot of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kir_Bulychov" title="Kir Bulychov" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBhI">Kir Bulychov</a>'s novella from the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alisa_Selezneva" title="Alisa Selezneva" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBhM">Alisa Selezneva</a> series, <i id="mwBhQ">Vacations in Space, or the Planet Five-Four</i>, is based on finding a secret base of mysterious "Wanderers" (Странники), an extinct highly advanced civilization. He also depicted his own Zone in the story <i id="mwBhU">Save Galya!</i></li>
<li id="mwBhY">In the late 1990s, a three-volume collection of fiction by notable contemporary Russian science fiction authors, titled <i id="mwBhc"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Time_of_the_Apprentices?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="The Time of the Apprentices (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" id="mwBhg" data-mw-i18n="{&quot;title&quot;:{&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;x-page&quot;,&quot;key&quot;:&quot;red-link-title&quot;,&quot;params&quot;:[&quot;The Time of the Apprentices&quot;]}}">The Time of the Apprentices</a></i> (Время учеников), was published with the endorsement of Boris Strugatsky. Each piece in the collection was a sequel to one of the Strugatskys' books.</li>
<li id="mwBhk">The asteroid <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3054_Strugatskia" title="3054 Strugatskia" id="mwBho">3054 Strugatskia</a>, discovered by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Stepanovich_Chernykh" title="Nikolai Stepanovich Chernykh" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBhs">Nikolai Stepanovich Chernykh</a> in 1977, was named after the Strugatsky brothers.</li>
<li id="mwBhw">The fictional moon <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fictional_universe_of_Avatar" title="Fictional universe of Avatar" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBh0">Pandora</a> depicted in the movie <i id="mwBh4"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avatar_(2009_film)" title="Avatar (2009 film)" id="mwBh8">Avatar</a></i> by James Cameron contains some similarities with the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noon_Universe" title="Noon Universe" id="mwBiA">Noon Universe</a> series, where a planet is also called Pandora.  Both are filled with jungle, where weird animals and a humanoid race live.  Also, the girlfriend of the biologist Sidorov in the Strugatskys' novel is called "Nava" (as compared with "Na'vi" as the name of the humanoid race in the film).  However, Boris Strugatsky rejected the idea that his works had been plagiarized, despite the similarities.<sup about="#mwt377" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Strygatsky_BVI_110-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;Strygatsky_BVI&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-Strygatsky_BVI-110&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-Strygatsky_BVI-110" id="mwBiE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBiI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBiM">[</span>100<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBiQ">]</span></span></a></sup></li>
<li id="mwBiU">In 2014 a square in Saint Petersburg was named after the Strugatsky brothers. A memorial museum is being opened in the same city.<sup about="#mwt379" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-111" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-111&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-111" id="mwBiY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBic"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBig">[</span>101<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBik">]</span></span></a></sup></li>
<li id="mwBio">The brothers are credited with saving humanity from mysterious "visitors" through technology retrieved from a "visitation zone" in the 2016 game <i id="mwBis"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Final_Station" title="The Final Station" id="mwBiw">The Final Station</a></i>.</li>
<li id="mwBi0">The Polish video game developer Acid Wizard Studio cited the Strugatskys as an influence on their 2017 game <i id="mwBi4"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darkwood" title="Darkwood" id="mwBi8">Darkwood</a></i>.<sup about="#mwt382" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-112" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-112&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-112" id="mwBjA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBjE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBjI">[</span>102<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBjM">]</span></span></a></sup></li>
<li id="mwBjQ">The designers of the 2019 video game <i id="mwBjU"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disco_Elysium" title="Disco Elysium" id="mwBjY">Disco Elysium</a></i> cited the Strugatskys' writing as an influence on the game's design and writing.<sup about="#mwt385" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-113" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-113&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-113" id="mwBjc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBjg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBjk">[</span>103<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBjo">]</span></span></a></sup></li>
<li id="mwBjs">The video game series <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S.T.A.L.K.E.R." title="S.T.A.L.K.E.R." id="mwBjw">S.T.A.L.K.E.R.</a> owes much of its background to the mix of the Strugatsky's writing and the Chernobyl disasters' zone of exclusion.</li></ul>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="25" id="mwBj0" aria-labelledby="Legacy_and_awards"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Legacy_and_awards">Legacy and awards</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Legacy and awards"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwBj4"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Boris_Strugacky_temporary_memorial_plaque.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwBj8"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Boris_Strugacky_temporary_memorial_plaque.jpg" src="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Boris_Strugacky_temporary_memorial_plaque.jpg/250px-Boris_Strugacky_temporary_memorial_plaque.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Boris_Strugacky_temporary_memorial_plaque.jpg/500px-Boris_Strugacky_temporary_memorial_plaque.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="1836" data-file-type="bitmap" height="141" width="250" class="mw-file-element" id="mwBkA"></a><figcaption id="mwBkE">Temporary memorial plaque in Saint Petersburg</figcaption></figure>
<p id="mwBkI">In 1959, the novella <i id="mwBkM">The Land of Crimson Clouds</i> won third prize in a competition for the best book on science and technology for schoolchildren, organized by the Ministry of Education of the RSFSR; the award was granted even before the book's publication. Until the early 1980s, the Strugatsky brothers received only public and amateur awards, such as the prize from the "Under the Integral" club (Novosibirsk Akademgorodok) for the best work about young scientists (1965, <i id="mwBkQ">Monday Begins on Saturday</i>) or the "Fant" awards from the KLF in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khabarovsk" title="Khabarovsk" id="mwBkU">Khabarovsk</a> for the novellas <i id="mwBkY">A Billion Years Before the End of the World</i> (1976) and <i id="mwBkc">Beetle in the Anthill</i> (1981). Various science fiction fan clubs regularly awarded the Strugatskys prizes in the following decade. After the establishment of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aelita_Prize" title="Aelita Prize" id="mwBkg"><i id="mwBkk">Aelita</i></a> award in 1981 by the editorial board of the magazine <i id="mwBko"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Pathfinder" title="Ural Pathfinder" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBks">Ural Pathfinder</a></i> and the Council for Adventure and Science Fiction Literature of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Writers_of_the_RSFSR" title="Union of Writers of the RSFSR" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBkw">Union of Writers of the RSFSR</a>, the Strugatskys became its first laureates for the novella <i id="mwBk0">Beetle in the Anthill</i>. In 1986, Arkady and Boris Strugatsky were awarded the RSFSR State Prize named after <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxim_Gorky" title="Maxim Gorky" id="mwBk4">Maxim Gorky</a>, and in the same year, B. N. Strugatsky shared the RSFSR State Prize named after the Vasilyev Brothers for the screenplay of the film <i id="mwBk8"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_from_a_Dead_Man" title="Letters from a Dead Man" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBlA">Letters from a Dead Man</a></i> with director <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_Lopushansky" title="Konstantin Lopushansky" id="mwBlE">Konstantin Lopushansky</a> and science fiction writer <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vyacheslav_Rybakov" title="Vyacheslav Rybakov" id="mwBlI">Vyacheslav Rybakov</a>. In 1988 and 1989, the writers received reader sympathy awards at the "Great Ring" festival. In 1990, the Strugatskys were honored with the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Belyaev" title="Alexander Belyaev" id="mwBlM">Alexander Belyaev</a> Literary Prize by the Leningrad Writers' Organization for the novel <i id="mwBlQ">Doomed City</i>. The posthumously published novella by A. Strugatsky (under the pseudonym "S. Yaroslavtsev"), <i id="mwBlU">Devil Among People</i>, received the "Interpresscon" award in 1994. In post-Soviet Russia, in 1999, Arkady (posthumously) and Boris Strugatsky were awarded the "Paladin of Science Fiction" prize by the Congress of Science Fiction Writers of Russia and the CIS, and in 2000, the <i id="mwBlY">Strannik</i> award in a similar category.<sup about="#mwt388" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-114" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-114&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-114" id="mwBlc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBlg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBlk">[</span>104<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBlo">]</span></span></a></sup> The Strugatsky brothers were laureates of the "Symbol of Science" medal for 2007.<sup about="#mwt391" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-115" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-115&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-115" id="mwBls"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBlw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBl0">[</span>105<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBl4">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwBl8">The Strugatsky brothers were among the few Soviet science fiction writers widely recognized and awarded by Western literary institutions and conventions.<sup about="#mwt392" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin19963–4_116-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin19963–4&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin19963–4-116&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;3–4&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin19963–4-116" id="mwBmA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBmE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBmI">[</span>106<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBmM">]</span></span></a></sup> In 1977, the English translation of the novella <i id="mwBmQ">Roadside Picnic</i> was honored with the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_W._Campbell_Memorial_Award_for_Best_Science_Fiction_Novel" title="John W. Campbell Memorial Award for Best Science Fiction Novel" id="mwBmU">John W. Campbell Memorial Award for Best Science Fiction Novel</a> by the "Science Fiction Writers of America" as the best work of the year. In 1978, the Strugatskys were elected honorary members of the Mark Twain Society (USA) "for outstanding contributions to world literature". In 1979, the brothers received the Jules Verne Prize from the Scandinavian Science Fiction Congress in Sweden, and in 1982, they were inducted into the <i id="mwBmY">Hall of Fame</i> at <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurocon" title="Eurocon" id="mwBmc">Eurocon</a> in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mönchengladbach" title="Mönchengladbach" id="mwBmg">Mönchengladbach</a>. <i id="mwBmk">Roadside Picnic</i> won the "Golden Demon" prize for the best foreign book at the Sixth French Science Fiction Festival in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metz" title="Metz" id="mwBmo">Metz</a> in 1981. The writers were recognized at Eurocon in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montpellier" title="Montpellier" id="mwBms">Montpellier</a> in 1987 and received a special award from the World Science Fiction Organization for "Independence of Thought" at the 45th Science Fiction Convention in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brighton" title="Brighton" id="mwBmw">Brighton</a>.<sup about="#mwt395" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin19963_117-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin19963&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin19963-117&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;sfn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Sfn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Amusin&quot;},&quot;2&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;1996&quot;},&quot;pp&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;3&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin19963-117" id="mwBm0"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBm4"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBm8">[</span>107<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBnA">]</span></span></a></sup> The Strugatskys were also inducted into the Hall of Fame at Eurocon in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freudenstadt" title="Freudenstadt" id="mwBnE">Freudenstadt</a> (1992).</p>

<p id="mwBnI">An <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid" title="Asteroid" id="mwBnM">asteroid</a>, No. 3054, discovered on 11 September 1977, at the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Astrophysical_Observatory" title="Crimean Astrophysical Observatory" id="mwBnQ">Crimean Astrophysical Observatory</a>, was named after the Strugatskys. The name was approved by the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_Planet_Center" title="Minor Planet Center" id="mwBnU">Minor Planet Center</a> in 1985.</p>

<p id="mwBnY">In 1994, the literary online contest "Teneta" was launched—the first in the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runet" title="Runet" id="mwBnc">Russian segment of the Internet.</a><sup about="#mwt400" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-118" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-118&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-118" id="mwBng"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBnk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBno">[</span>108<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBns">]</span></span></a></sup> Boris Strugatsky participated in the jury of the 1997 contest, sparking criticism for his judging results (notably the "discovery" of the marginal writer Bayan Shiryanov).<sup about="#mwt403" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-119" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-119&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-119" id="mwBnw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBn0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBn4">[</span>109<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBn8">]</span></span></a></sup> The writer himself stated that the level of the contest works he reviewed was "professional (indistinguishable from the average level of a typical modern magazine)," rating it higher than the writers' seminar he led. However, Boris Natanovich believed that "network literature, of course, has no special future and cannot have one by definition".<sup about="#mwt406" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-120" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-120&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-120" id="mwBoA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBoE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBoI">[</span>110<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBoM">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt409" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-121" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-121&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-121" id="mwBoQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBoU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBoY">[</span>111<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBoc">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwBog">Since 1998, the literary ABS Prize has been awarded, established personally by B. N. Strugatsky. Shortly before his death in 2012, the Strugatsky Brothers Foundation was created, with S. Arno as its executive director and administrator of the prize. In 2024, the awarding of the prize was suspended due to Andrei Strugatsky (son of Boris Strugatsky) stepping down as chairman of the Foundation, as well as the death of the ABS Prize's executive secretary, N. M. Romanetsky.<sup about="#mwt412" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-122" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-122&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-122" id="mwBok"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBoo"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBos">[</span>112<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBow">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwBo0">In 2011, the Mikhail Prokhorov Foundation, which established the literary prize "NOS," launched the "NOS-1973" project, which considered texts written or first published in 1973. The shortlist, among works by fifteen writers (including <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Nabokov" title="Vladimir Nabokov" id="mwBo4">Nabokov</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Trifonov" title="Yuri Trifonov" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBo8">Trifonov</a>), included the Strugatskys' novella <i id="mwBpA">Roadside Picnic.</i><sup about="#mwt418" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-123" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-123&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-123" id="mwBpE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBpI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBpM">[</span>113<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBpQ">]</span></span></a></sup> Jury member Vladislav Tolstov,<sup about="#mwt413" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-124" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-124&quot;},&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;refn&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Refn&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;},&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Vladislav Alexandrovich Tolstov (born 1968) – Russian journalist. Worked in editorial offices in Norilsk, Krasnoyarsk, Irkutsk, Moscow, and elsewhere. Author of several books on the history of Norilsk, Eastern Siberia, and the Upper Volga region.&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><a href="#cite_note-124" data-mw-group="Notes" id="mwBpU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBpY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBpc">[</span>Notes 11<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBpg">]</span></span></a></sup> explaining the discrepancy between the jury's choice and reader voting on the prize's website (the jury selected <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Sinyavsky" title="Andrei Sinyavsky" id="mwBpk">Sinyavsky</a>'s <i id="mwBpo">Strolls with Pushkin</i>, experts chose <i id="mwBps"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow-Petushki" title="Moscow-Petushki" id="mwBpw">Moscow-Petushki</a>,</i> and online voters picked <i id="mwBp0"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandro_of_Chegem" title="Sandro of Chegem" id="mwBp4">Sandro of Chegem</a></i>), noted:</p>

<blockquote id="mwBp8">
<p id="mwBqA">...from the nauseatingly official literary lineup that was Soviet literature in 1973, the books by the Strugatskys, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fazil_Iskander" title="Fazil Iskander" id="mwBqE">Iskander</a>, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasily_Shukshin" title="Vasily Shukshin" id="mwBqI">Shukshin</a> stand out above all. Because they were read, exchanged, stolen from libraries, cut out from thick magazines, and rebound. Discussed. Remembered. That's important. But <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Sinyavsky" title="Andrei Sinyavsky" id="mwBqM">Terts</a> — they don't remember. They just don't, and that's it. And they don't reprint him for that reason.</p>
</blockquote>

<p id="mwBqQ">Jury member Aleksey Levinson noted that the selected books "did not distance readers from life but allowed them to live it, making it acceptable. They played an interesting role in reconciling the reader with their everyday reality." The Strugatskys' Roadside Picnic was called "hyperreal," while the world of Chegem was an "extrareality." In any case, these books (including the "alcoholic world" of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venedikt_Erofeev" title="Venedikt Erofeev" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBqU">V. Erofeev</a>) defended a system of true values, distinguishing them "from pulp fiction that leads to a world of values the reader cannot and will not adhere to in life".<sup about="#mwt421" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-125" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-125&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-125" id="mwBqY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBqc"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBqg">[</span>114<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBqk">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwBqo">In April 2024, a presentation was held for Vasily Vladimirsky's project "A World Without the Strugatskys". The brief given to the anthology's authors was roughly as follows:</p>

<blockquote id="mwBqs">
<p id="mwBqw">The Strugatskys never existed, but modern Russian science fiction emerged nonetheless. Which Soviet non-science fiction writers could have acted as its founders? Conceive and write a science fiction text for that author.</p>
</blockquote>

<p id="mwBq0">The results of the experiment elicited mixed reactions from critics.<sup about="#mwt424" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-126" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-126&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-126" id="mwBq4"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBq8"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBrA">[</span>115<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBrE">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt427" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-127" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-127&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-127" id="mwBrI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBrM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBrQ">[</span>116<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBrU">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwBrY">In 2014, a square in Saint Petersburg, near Boris Strugatsky's home, was named after the writers.<sup about="#mwt430" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-128" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-128&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-128" id="mwBrc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBrg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBrk">[</span>117<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBro">]</span></span></a></sup> In 2015, plans were made to create a museum in Boris Strugatsky's Saint Petersburg apartment,<sup about="#mwt433" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Росбалт_129-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;Росбалт&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-Росбалт-129&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-Росбалт-129" id="mwBrs"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBrw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBr0">[</span>118<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBr4">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt436" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-130" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-130&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-130" id="mwBr8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBsA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBsE">[</span>119<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBsI">]</span></span></a></sup> but by 2023, the exact opening date and location of the museum remained unknown.<sup about="#mwt439" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-131" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-131&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-131" id="mwBsM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBsQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBsU">[</span>120<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBsY">]</span></span></a></sup> In 2023, a memorial plaque was installed on the building in Saint Petersburg on Pobedy Street, where Boris Strugatsky lived.<sup about="#mwt442" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-132" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-132&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-132" id="mwBsc"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBsg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBsk">[</span>121<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBso">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="26" id="mwBss" aria-labelledby="See_also"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="See_also">See also</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: See also"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>

<li id="mwBtE"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strugatsky's_high_theory_of_upbringing" title="Strugatsky's high theory of upbringing" id="mwBtI">Strugatsky's high theory of upbringing</a></li></ul>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="27" id="mwBtM" aria-labelledby="Notes"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Notes">Notes</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Notes"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt445" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwBtQ" data-mw="{&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;reflist&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Reflist&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1327269900" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" about="#mwt446" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;templatestyles&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;src&quot;:&quot;Reflist/styles.css&quot;}}">.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}body.skin-vector-2022 .mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:27em}body.skin-vector-2022 .mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:22.5em}.mw-parser-output .references[data-mw-group=upper-alpha]{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .references[data-mw-group=upper-roman]{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .references[data-mw-group=lower-alpha]{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .references[data-mw-group=lower-greek]{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .references[data-mw-group=lower-roman]{list-style-type:lower-roman}.mw-parser-output div.reflist-liststyle-upper-alpha .references{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output div.reflist-liststyle-upper-roman .references{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output div.reflist-liststyle-lower-alpha .references{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output div.reflist-liststyle-lower-greek .references{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output div.reflist-liststyle-lower-roman .references{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style></span><div about="#mwt445" id="mwBtU">
<div class="mw-references-wrap mw-references-columns" typeof="mw:Extension/references" about="#mwt447" id="mwBtY" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;references&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;Notes&quot;,&quot;responsive&quot;:&quot;1&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;&quot;}}"><ol class="mw-references references" data-mw-group="Notes" id="mwBtc"><li about="#cite_note-13" id="cite_note-13" data-mw-footnote-number="1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwBtg"><a href="#cite_ref-13" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBtk" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBto">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-13" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">Biographers and critics have repeatedly noted the difference in quality between the Strugatskys' literary works created in various authorship formats. Ant Skalandis, characterizing the debut novella in three stories <i id="mwBts">From the inside</i> (1960), said it was written by three different people: the first chapter by Arkady Strugatsky, the second by Boris Strugatsky, and the third by "a very young, very inexperienced writer" — the Strugatsky Brothers.<sup about="#mwt44" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008202_11-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTESkalandis2008202&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008202-11&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008202-11" id="mwBtw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBt0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBt4">[</span>11<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBt8">]</span></span></a></sup> Boris Mezhyev also stated: "Everything written by S. Yaroslavtsev (i.e., <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkady_Natanovich_Strugatsky" title="Arkady Natanovich Strugatsky" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBuA">Arkady Natanovich</a> alone) was, on the whole, an order of magnitude lower than the work of <abbr title="Abbreviation used by the co-authors: &quot;Arkady and Boris Strugatsky&quot;." about="#mwt48" typeof="mw:ExpandedAttrs" id="mwBuI" data-mw="{&quot;attribs&quot;:[[{&quot;txt&quot;:&quot;title&quot;},{&quot;html&quot;:&quot;&lt;span typeof=\&quot;mw:Nowiki\&quot; id=\&quot;mwBuE\&quot;&gt;Abbreviation used by the co-authors: \&quot;Arkady and Boris Strugatsky\&quot;.&lt;/span&gt;&quot;}]]}">ABS</abbr>, while what was written by S. Vititsky (i.e., <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Natanovich_Strugatsky" title="Boris Natanovich Strugatsky" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBuM">Boris Natanovich</a> alone) was no weaker than the best works of the great brothers. Ant Skalandis described the individual works of A. N. and B. N. Strugatsky in similar terms.<sup about="#mwt46" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008622_12-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTESkalandis2008622&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008622-12&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008622-12" id="mwBuQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBuU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBuY">[</span>12<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBuc">]</span></span></a></sup></span></li>
<li about="#cite_note-15" id="cite_note-15" data-mw-footnote-number="2"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwBug"><a href="#cite_ref-15" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBuk" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBuo">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-15" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">Boris Strugatsky also categorically stated that the eventful side of their biography with his brother concluded in 1956.The realization of a unified writerly persona prompted the brothers to calculate its "birth date," equidistant from 1925 and 1933; they arrived at 1929.</span></li>
<li about="#cite_note-36" id="cite_note-36" data-mw-footnote-number="3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwBus"><a href="#cite_ref-36" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBuw" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBu0">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-36" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">In Moscow's literary life in the 1950s, informal associations like salons played a significant role; A. N. Strugatsky was initially a member of translator <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vera_Nikolaevna_Markova?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="Vera Nikolaevna Markova (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" id="mwBu4" data-mw-i18n="{&quot;title&quot;:{&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;x-page&quot;,&quot;key&quot;:&quot;red-link-title&quot;,&quot;params&quot;:[&quot;Vera Nikolaevna Markova&quot;]}}">Vera Markova</a>’s salon, where Roman Kim was a regular and where the acquaintance with Yefremov occurred.<sup about="#mwt123" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008118_34-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTESkalandis2008118&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008118-34&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkalandis2008118-34" id="mwBu8"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBvA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBvE">[</span>32<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBvI">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt125" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016131_35-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016131&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016131-35&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016131-35" id="mwBvM"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBvQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBvU">[</span>33<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBvY">]</span></span></a></sup></span></li>
<li about="#cite_note-40" id="cite_note-40" data-mw-footnote-number="4"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwBvc"><a href="#cite_ref-40" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBvg" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBvk">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-40" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">In the 1970s, science fiction split into "official" and "samizdat" categories. The former existed under the control of the Central Committee of the CPSU, with official publication and distribution channels being the journal <i id="mwBvo">Iskatel</i> and the new editorial board of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molodaya_Gvardiya_(publisher)" title="Molodaya Gvardiya (publisher)" id="mwBvs">Molodaya Gvardiya</a> led by Yuri Medvedev.<sup about="#mwt144" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016269–272_39-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016269–272&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016269–272-39&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChernyakhovskaya2016269–272-39" id="mwBvw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBv0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBv4">[</span>36<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBv8">]</span></span></a></sup></span></li>
<li about="#cite_note-43" id="cite_note-43" data-mw-footnote-number="5"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwBwA"><a href="#cite_ref-43" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBwE" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBwI">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-43" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">Critic Roman Arbitman wrote in the preface to the 33-volume complete works: "In reality, the writers were neither ardent dissidents — despite 'The Ugly Swans' published by the émigré 'Posev' — nor, even less, pillars of the ruling regime — despite the consistent presence of the words 'communist' and 'communard' in their novels about a bright future. The writers simply tried to live honestly, without compromising themselves, within the given conditions and extracted the maximum from the few degrees of freedom granted to them at the time by the Homeostatic Universe".</span></li>
<li about="#cite_note-49" id="cite_note-49" data-mw-footnote-number="6"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwBwM"><a href="#cite_ref-49" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBwQ" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBwU">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-49" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">In A. N. Strugatsky's diary entry dated 20 January 1991, he wrote: "Tired of Boris. It feels like he comes to gorge on meat and watch videos and TV. Toward the end, we quarreled. &lt;...&gt; I was beside myself with anger. I can't deal with him anymore. He makes me ill. He plans to show up in the second half of February, but I won't let him. &lt;...&gt; B. N., my health is more important than you. After his departure, I even had something like a breakdown..."</span></li>
<li about="#cite_note-61" id="cite_note-61" data-mw-footnote-number="7"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwBwY"><a href="#cite_ref-61" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBwc" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBwg">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-61" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">Conversely, D. Volodihin and G. Prashkevich, characterizing the stylistics of their work, stated that early Yefremov is as much more dynamic and lively than the early Strugatskys as the mature Strugatskys (from the mid-1960s) are literarily more refined than the mature Yefremov.</span></li>
<li about="#cite_note-66" id="cite_note-66" data-mw-footnote-number="8"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwBwk"><a href="#cite_ref-66" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBwo" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBws">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-66" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">Unlike Lem and Le Guin, the Strugatskys never attempted to model alternative biological forms of intelligent life or a consistent picture of a society built on such a biological basis. With rare exceptions, their aliens are indistinguishable from humans in biosocial terms, differing only in the flow and forms of historical time. The only complete, consistent depiction of an extraterrestrial civilization is presented in the forest chapters of "Snail on the Slope." M. Amusin noted that Le Guin and most of her colleagues focused readers' attention on the "distant," exotic, and incomprehensible. Her Ekumen is simultaneously characterized by centripetal force and atomization; the worldview is conveyed through complex mythopoetic metaphors and intricate symbolism. The Strugatskys' universe, in worldview terms, is Ptolemaic—it gravitates toward and revolves around humanity. Their writerly approach is fundamentally anti-transcendental and anti-mythological, lacking references to higher, universal entities; the most complex problems are conceived as intellectually graspable, tangible, and observable.<sup about="#mwt229" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996134–135_65-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEAmusin1996134–135&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996134–135-65&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmusin1996134–135-65" id="mwBww"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwBw0"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBw4">[</span>58<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBw8">]</span></span></a></sup></span></li>
<li about="#cite_note-71" id="cite_note-71" data-mw-footnote-number="9"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwBxA"><a href="#cite_ref-71" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBxE" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBxI">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-71" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">The question of the Strugatskys' texts' addressivity is considered separately, as the authors themselves rarely indicated it (the only exception being the ironic subtitle to "Monday Begins on Saturday"— "for junior scientific researchers"). In "Lame Fate," there is a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deconstruction" title="Deconstruction" id="mwBxM">deconstruction</a> of the reader's image: Felix Sorokin claims he knows nothing about his readers. "They are just a lot of unfamiliar, completely extraneous people to me." Virtually all comprehensive studies of the Strugatskys' work include periodizations based on the observation that works written in different years differ so significantly that they seem addressed to different audiences. Most researchers find the Strugatskys interesting only after they moved beyond "hard science fiction" (<i id="mwBxQ">hard SF</i>). <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lev_Losev" title="Lev Losev" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBxU">Lev Losev</a> noted (without referencing the Strugatskys' texts) that under the guise of children's or young adult literature, a message for far more sophisticated readers might be concealed.</span></li>
<li about="#cite_note-74" id="cite_note-74" data-mw-footnote-number="10"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwBxY"><a href="#cite_ref-74" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBxc" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBxg">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-74" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">Interpretations of allusions and allegories often prove unworkable or misleading. "Doctor <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golem" title="Golem" id="mwBxk">Golem</a> in <i id="mwBxo"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ugly_Swans" title="The Ugly Swans" id="mwBxs">The Ugly Swans</a></i> (1967) has absolutely no connection to the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabbalah" title="Kabbalah" id="mwBxw">Kabbalistic</a> legend, and the names of characters in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Invasion_from_Mars?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="Second Invasion from Mars (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" id="mwBx0" data-mw-i18n="{&quot;title&quot;:{&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;x-page&quot;,&quot;key&quot;:&quot;red-link-title&quot;,&quot;params&quot;:[&quot;Second Invasion from Mars&quot;]}}">Second Invasion from Mars</a> (1966) are arbitrarily borrowed from <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_mythology" title="Ancient Greek mythology" class="mw-redirect" id="mwBx4">Greek mythology</a>: an aging housewife has nothing in common with the mythological <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurydice" title="Eurydice" id="mwBx8">Eurydice</a>, a city intellectual with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charon" title="Charon" id="mwByA">Charon</a>, a tedious pharmacist with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achilles" title="Achilles" id="mwByE">Achilles</a>, and so on. Unlike <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Joyce" title="James Joyce" id="mwByI">Joyce</a>'s <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulysses_(novel)" title="Ulysses (novel)" id="mwByM"><i id="mwByQ">Ulysses</i></a> or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Updike" title="John Updike" id="mwByU">Updike</a>'s <i id="mwByY">Centaur</i>, <i id="mwByc">Second Invasion from Mars</i> does not imply characterological or plot-related hints. Readers encounter a pseudo-puzzle and simultaneously something recognized as a particular 'atmosphere,' 'aura' of the narrative. The ancient Greek aura may introduce meanings of fate, choice, and tragedy, contrasting with the grotesque story of Martians harvesting human gastric juice on an industrial scale, but it resists further decryption".<sup about="#mwt262" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKaspe_70-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;group&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;name&quot;:&quot;FOOTNOTEKaspe&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaspe-70&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaspe-70" id="mwByg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwByk"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwByo">[</span>62<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwBys">]</span></span></a></sup></span></li>
<li about="#cite_note-124" id="cite_note-124" data-mw-footnote-number="11"><span class="mw-cite-backlink" id="mwByw"><a href="#cite_ref-124" data-mw-group="Notes" rel="mw:referencedBy" id="mwBy0" aria-label="Jump up" title="Jump up"><span class="mw-linkback-text" id="mwBy4">↑</span></a></span> <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-124" class="mw-reference-text reference-text" data-mw-group="Notes">Vladislav Alexandrovich Tolstov (born 1968) – Russian journalist. Worked in editorial offices in Norilsk, Krasnoyarsk, Irkutsk, Moscow, and elsewhere. Author of several books on the history of Norilsk, Eastern Siberia, and the Upper Volga region.</span></li>
</ol></div></div>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="28" id="mwBy8" aria-labelledby="References"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="References">References</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arkady_and_Boris_Strugatsky&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: References"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
